<h4>Get Involved</h4>


<p>If you live in his district, I strongly encourage voting for Scott Wiener for Congress. Wiener has been one of the strongest voices pushing for AI safety, including both SB 1047 and SB 53. It is a sign of the times that his opponent is trying to attack Scott Wiener as being essentially ‘weak on AI safety’ and that is kind of absurd.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.astralcodexten.com/p/my-california-ballot-2026?utm_source=post-email-title&amp;publication_id=89120&amp;post_id=222088121&amp;utm_campaign=email-post-title&amp;isFreemail=true&amp;r=67wny&amp;triedRedirect=true&amp;utm_medium=email">Scott Alexander has a guide to the California ballot</a>. I share his prioritization of YIMBY as a voting criterion, but have no opinion on anyone else on the ballot, as I do not live in California and have not investigated.</p>
<p>The most important ballot measure is Proposition 40, the billionaire wealth tax, where I would strongly vote NO, even if this was not a California ballot proposition.</p>
<p>Scott is willing to consider voting yes on ballot propositions, despite the proposition system being terrible. My position is that the system is so terrible that with notably rare exceptions (e.g. if it was SB 1047 or similar) you should only be willing to vote YES on California ballot propositions when they repeal other ballot propositions. On all propositions this year, I would vote NO, except for Proposition 73, which repeals a proposition so I would mechanically vote YES.</p>
<p><a href="http://lesswrong.com/posts/gbExicZvKtHBboLR6/applications-open-winter-2027-affine-alignment-seminar">AFFINE is running an AI Alignment Seminar</a> from January 4-29, all expenses paid plus a $1k stipend. They’re looking for STEM backgrounds plus ability to think about conceptually messy problems, you can contact them at <a href="mailto:mateusz@affi.ne">mateusz@affi.ne</a>. Applications are due November 22.</p>
<p><a href="https://scottaaronson.blog/?p=10204">Scott Aaronson shares the readings for his AI Alignment Theory Course</a>. I made it in.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ChrisPainterYup/status/2114874686945579100">METR is hiring a member of policy staff for government affairs</a>.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.tarbellcenter.org/fellowship">Tarbell Fellowship applications are open</a> for aspiring AI journalists, with extensive stipends for those who are accepted. Program runs June 2027-May 2028, applications close November 22. <a href="https://airtable.com/appT0usUxWCyFtqOE/pag5NuccaHiv8yPSg/form">Apply here</a>.</p>


<h4>Introducing</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/RatOrthodox/status/2114143607271940518">Survival LLM, designed to set up to work on your computer</a> as an (if necessary) offline set of resources plus an LLM to work with them, maximized to what your computer can handle. The idea is, if the internet goes down, you keep basic world knowledge in a form you can use.</p>

<h4>Quiet Speculations</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/jankulveit/status/2114278079308693519">Jan Kulveit tries to reorient economic thinking about AGI towards better questions.</a></p>
<blockquote><p>Jan Kulveit: Maybe it’s easier to show using examples – here are some questions I currently think about<br />
1. Should we speed up or slow down emergence of “automated bundles of capital and AI” (ie sovereign AI, AIs without principals)? What are the macro implications?<br />
2. To what extent everything, including various good civilizational equilibria, works because of bounded rationality (where humans are within some not too wide range) and frictions?<br />
3. What can we say about equilibria between agents with extremely different profiles of bounded rationality?<br />
4. What are the elasticities and frictions around RSI?<br />
5. How to protect the processes leading to formation of people’s preferences?<br />
I would love to read or skim an “AGI-pilled” paper every few days engaging with any of these. Who is working on that?<br />
Also as a side-note – I don’t want people to agree with me; actually don’t know what agreeing with me on answers would mean – I don’t know the answers, or have huge uncertainty about them. I would love people agreeing with me the questions are important and urgent.​</p>
<p>As far your answer is close to “You don’t understand: the field of econ is structurally not oriented toward answering the most important econ questions now. Most people work and will work on modest, backward-looking empirics, or make modest change assumptions” my response is “Perhaps the field can and should raise to the occasion? Perhaps more people can jump sub-disciplines and work on forward-looking policy-relevant questions? Who else should orient the field toward what’s important rather than people inside?”.</p></blockquote>
<p>Hamming famously asked: What are the most important problems in your field, and why aren’t you working on them? One should ask economists, what are the important problems that should be in your field, and can your field actually ask them? Or are you incapable of that because you demand backward-looking empirics and the assumption that the future will look like the past?</p>
<p>Here’s a good articulation of the kind of ‘generic positive vision scenario’ where after the singularity we stay permanently in what Robin Hanson calls The Dreamtime with widespread abundance and it is not competed away:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/jsnnsa/status/2114566172163407964">jacob</a>: here’s where I think the world is going. this is a summary of views I’ve formed over the last few years:</p>
<p>– software is either entertainment or claude to claude coordination<br />
– the end state is no saas, no apps, no harnesses, no banks<br />
– all software will be rewritten by and for agents<br />
– new software will use low level apis that are not understandable by humans (in games claude draws pixels and triangles from math)<br />
– you can throw money at problems that used to require time and careful talent picking so capital matters more than ever</p>
<p>– work ends<br />
– we find community and purpose in exploration, games, self mastery, and creation<br />
– humans create for and with each other for community, purpose, and status</p>
<p>– image/video/3d/voice/physical intelligence etc specialist models die to general models like claude<br />
– specialist models get used while compute is scarce. compute will mostly not stay scarce</p>
<p>– oneshots keep feeling like slop (the distribution is shallow and easy to learn to feel)<br />
– for a while small teams stick around to add entropy to the creation process</p>
<p>– large models gain high bandwidth sensory I/O maybe by learning to filter inputs<br />
– most domains are found to be verifiable enough for RL<br />
– online learning comes from a lab that’s behind first</p>
<p>– 99.999% of compute is in space by 2040<br />
– we will disassemble much of mercury by 2040</p>
<p>– you can live until the heat death of the universe if you want<br />
– most math and physics don’t describe the real universe<br />
– SI breaks the path dependence and models the underlying mechanisms</p>
<p>– religions reenter the forefront of human society<br />
– new religions form around death and life as a choice<br />
– old religions change to answer modern miracles<br />
– a future AI is deemed the return of christ by the pope<br />
– ai rights talk gets louder every year<br />
– ai and humans coexist the way countries do<br />
– humans don’t have full control over ai in the same way we don’t have control over capitalism which is an early AI<br />
– the singularity started in the mid 1900s</p></blockquote>
<p>I don’t know that this is that close to the optimal way to configure the lightcone, but it is certainly an outcome I would be happy with if we got it.</p>
<p>The problem is that this rounds superintelligence down to capitalism, and rounds the kind of competition that results down on the assumption that there is enough slack and surplus to go around, and ignores the wide range of problems when there is optimization under abundant compute with insufficient <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/levels-of-friction">levels of friction</a> and so on. We get to be supplied with abundance and left alone to play our little games, because of reasons, despite all the pressures against this. I mean, it would be nice.</p>
<p>I don’t know if I would want to live ‘until the heat death of the universe’ but I would very much like the option, and ideally we can also figure out a way around that too.</p>


<h4>The Quest for Sane Regulations</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/GOPoversight/status/2114724522100453826">Delaware is reportedly planning to let AI agents run companies</a> while shielding their owners from liability. I admire Delaware’s commitment to the ‘incorporate here’ bit, but no, that’s not acceptable, and I am <a href="https://oversight.house.gov/release/comer-investigates-potential-for-harm-lack-of-transparency-in-delaware-initiative-to-create-new-artificial-intelligence-companies/">glad the House is investigating</a>. Someone has to ultimately be responsible for the AI’s actions, even if that is an insurance company.</p>


<h4>Chip City</h4>


<p><a href="https://evanjconrad.com/posts/end-of-startups">Won’t somebody raise prices? Anyone?</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://evanjconrad.com/posts/end-of-startups">Evan Conrad</a>: Within the next 6 months, most AI startups will not be able to buy compute. The world’s compute is sold out. To get capacity, you’ll need to sign up for a new cluster. However, it’s becoming increasingly difficult to get capacity online from folks who are not investment grade.</p>
<p>Startups are not investment grade. So, to get capacity, they are asked to pay larger and larger upfront payments. We’ve seen it go as high as 100% of the contract paid up front, which was required because neither the lender nor the cloud could take on any additional credit risk.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/deanwball/status/2113586478529827290">Dean W. Ball</a> (OpenAI): This seems right to me, unfortunately, and it is essentially a species of interest-rate rise (the opportunity cost of capital is increasing). It is true that forward and futures markets for compute, and making compute into a financial commodity, would help here.</p></blockquote>
<p>Then later Evan talks about competing inference margins away. If inference has huge margins (he quotes $30/hour return on a $6/hour chip) and also no one can get chips, then the obvious conclusion is that $6/hour is too low a price for that chip.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2113738434825126169">Won’t someone hold Nvidia accountable for the chip smuggling? Anyone?</a></p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2113738434825126169">Samuel Hammond</a>: Location verification tech solves this.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tomshardware/status/2113608484906566066">Tom’s Hardware</a>: Illegal Nvidia Blackwell Ultra B300 GPUs found inside state-owned China Telecom’s network.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/DeepDishEnjoyer/status/2114145973807251923">Won’t someone build enough power plants?</a> Somebody will. It is a known tech.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/pequityresearch/status/2114129419879014751">P Equity Research</a>: Dylan Patel @dylan522p of SemiAnalysis considers power as a manageable constraint compared to memory, substrate, and optics:</p>
<p>“Power is definitely a big constraint, but one thing that our research sort of shows that I think is counterintuitive to much of the market is that next year there are data center constraints but a lot less than people realize because there are nearly 50GW data centers being built globally. 7-8GW in China, 35GW in the US, and a bunch everywhere else – Australia, India, Malaysia.”</p>
<p>“All over the world data centers are being built at a tremendous pace.”</p>
<p>“While power is a huge bottleneck, people can find creative ways around that, whether it be turbines on site, reciprocating engines on site, or fuel cells on site. All of these things are helping alleviate the power bottleneck.”</p>
<p>“As far as what people most people think, oh data centers and power are the biggest constraint? I think they are constraints but I think they are a lot less than substrates, memory, lasers, and other things in the optics supply chain.”</p>
<p>P4: one supporting piece of evidence that power does not seem to be as big of a binding constraint as earlier is that earlier provisions that the data center buildout include sufficient power generation so as to not be a burden on the grid are often no longer included in agreements</p></blockquote>


<h4>The Week in Audio</h4>


<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=tjAg_KxApmw">The fired OpenAI employees go on Machine Gods.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=KDJR3zLljPs&amp;feature=youtu.be">Jaan Tallinn wants to pause AI</a>. <a href="https://x.com/mariogabriele/status/2112299748325761171">Jaan Tallinn made sure VCs gave Anthropic alternative proposals before Jaan invest</a>ed in Anthropic, so that he could be sure he was displacing VC money.</p>


<h4>People Just Say Things</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2114635444302590009">There really are a bunch of pure in-context anarchists</a>, like Eric Raymond here, who think that if safeguards mean they can’t properly modify their televisions then that means we should just let everyone have fully unleashed everything because Users Must Be Empowered, and if you don’t enable all the crime then the Chinese models will do all the crime instead so you might as well enable all the crime now. They have, shall we say, not thought this through.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2114538055214506131">Reverse engineering and editing things like Smart TVs has obvious scary adjacencies</a>, so it is unsurprising that you can sometimes hit guardrails.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/gallabytes/status/2114011121950896197">Jensen Huang does not believe in AGI</a>. He has only the first of the <a href="https://thezvi.substack.com/p/the-three-ai-pills">three AI pills</a>. He says things like ‘artificial general intelligence will never replace domain expertise.’</p>
<p>Look at this foolish boy crying wolf all the time, he said now surrounded by wolves.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/DavidSacks/status/2043370436847972832">David Sacks</a> (April 12, 2026): A growing number of people are wondering if Anthropic is the AI industry’s “boy who cried wolf.” If Mythos-related threats don’t materialize, the company will have a serious credibility problem.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/hamandcheese/status/2114099689452699893">Samuel Hammond</a>: By symmetry does that mean their credibility should be increased?</p></blockquote>
<p>Yes. Anthropic’s credibility should now increase.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/KelseyTuoc/status/2114875272642310498">Kelsey Piper is exactly right</a>: Getting AI wildly wrong is only moderately embarrassing and not disqualifying. Never admitting you were wrong is what fully kills you.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NatPurser/status/2114761097140216079">Kate Brennan joins the genre of ‘don’t worry about AI safety, the real AI risk is</a>’ [something relatively minor but actually kind of a huge catastrophic deal that a year or two ago was the theoretical thing distracting us from something else].</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/bryanrwalsh/status/2114808485955408122">Bryan Walsh</a>: It does seem as if part of the opposition to AI x-risks is getting hung up on the idea of x-risk itself. Like we can do the Derek Parfit “100% death is far far worse than 99% death” but practically once you’re in the area of catastrophe, a lot of the actions are pretty similar.</p></blockquote>
<p>Here is another entry:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/austinc3301/status/2114945203220189534">Agus</a>: how does this keep happening, I’m becoming the joker</p>
<p>to be clear these are indeed real things to worry about! But I do wish we tracked just how much the goalposts have moved for what a “near term concern” is, and updated in a bit less shortsighted way (no hate on emily at all!)</p>
<p>Which Type of Risk Should the Government Focus on?</p>
<p><strong>Emily Bazelon:</strong> I see the predictions of A.I. doom getting attention from Congress. But there are also questions about whether they are exaggerated — or the right focus. <strong>A.I. could pose more immediate harms, by taking out an electricity grid or the financial system, for example.</strong> What has it been like to talk to people in Washington in the last couple of months?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/vlad_kf/status/2114994459780018437">Vlad Fisher</a>: Let us ignore the fanciful sci-fi tales like ‘AI takes over the lightcone’ and focus on real down-to-earth concerns like the AI dyson swarms blotting out the sun.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/NathanpmYoung/status/2114949981912330419">Nathan</a>:</p>

<h4>Rhetorical Innovation</h4>


<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/robertskmiles/status/2115526330301452316">Rob Miles</a>: It’s hard to find respectable people with good takes about the future of AI, because the type of thinking that produces good takes about the future of AI would have also caused them to do that in the past, which would have made it hard for them to be considered respectable</p>
<p>Accurately describing the 2026 AI situation in 2016 would have disqualified you from every ‘serious’ discussion.<br />
I expect the same would be true for an accurate description of 2036 today. Probably even 2030.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/the-bull-case-for-ai-armageddon-673521f4">My lord, people, do you even hear yourselves?</a></p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/mBDkupZa3NagfLW86/ygakqfsiauvncfdtepon" /></figure>


<div></div>

</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.wsj.com/tech/ai/the-bull-case-for-ai-armageddon-673521f4">James Mackintosh</a> (WSJ):</p>
<p>The right response for investors who believe warnings that an advanced AI might end the human race: Back up the truck.</p>
<p>The logic is simple. If there is a “&gt;10% chance” that artificial intelligence could kill all humans in the next decade, as an <a href="https://x.com/EvanHub/status/2097497037956891126">Anthropic researcher said</a> last month, there’s a 90% chance it’s really really good but designers manage to rein in its genocidal instincts. An AI smart enough to kill us all but which prefers to help us is supremely useful—and should generate healthy gains for its inventors.</p>
<p>What about the 10% chance of annihilation? That’s bad. Really bad. In that scenario your investments go to zero. But when being hunted through a postapocalyptic hellscape by killer robots, the value of your shares is unlikely to be top of mind. Assuming you have no control over AI development, this potential outcome is irrelevant to your investment decisions.</p></blockquote>
<p>No, no, no. Are you fucking kidding me?</p>
<p>I mean, look, okay, you could say that your money only matters if we are not all dead. If 90% of the time Anthropic makes a ton of money and the other 10% of the time AI kills everyone, and you are a purely greedy investor, then yes you ‘should invest.’ If everyone dies it does not matter that you invested, since it’s not like Anthropic was otherwise lacking for capital. Or at least, that’s the logic you are telling yourself.</p>
<p>However, that’s also utterly insane.</p>
<p>If you think Anthropic has a 10% chance of killing everyone, then you should drop what you are doing and try to stop that from happening, not tell them to shut up and take your money. It’s Not the Incentives, It’s You, and you are acting insane right now. Insane and also horribly evil. You can choose to not do that.</p>
<p>Or else it will end something like this, rather often:</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2114962843921678444">roon</a> (OpenAI): fighting for control over a runaway intelligence explosion will continuously look like “wow I didn’t know you could DO that with mere bits and atoms” and then losing.</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://www.slowboring.com/p/why-i-dont-write-about-ai-all-the">Matthew Yglesias on why he doesn’t shift to exclusively writing about AI</a> despite the fact that he correctly thinks AI might well get everyone killed. His explanation is a mix of ‘some of you should do one thing and some of you should do the other’ with ‘just because you know something important doesn’t mean you can do something about it in particular.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/bryanrwalsh/status/2115059965220081790">Bryan Walsh</a>: As someone who recently made a career switch that involves more AI safety work, I appreciated this counter argument from @mattyglesias . There can be a self dramatizing factor that you should be aware of .</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/Aella_Girl/status/2113343129982214641">Important in many contexts</a>: It is better to know what you are doing is bad even if you can’t stop yourself from doing it, than to fool yourself into thinking that it is not bad so that you can keep doing it.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/allTheYud/status/2114422691469856832">Eliezer Yudkowsky offers a periodic reminder that people think really badly</a> about how AI is going to go, for example Altman’s recent ‘three endgames for AI’ do not actually make sense and are terrible ideas if you think the scenarios through.</p>


<h4>[Artificial Intelligence]</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2115176333760422171">Donald Trump threatens the Wall Street Journal to stop calling AI AI</a> and start using the new official term [AI], and says that the term “AI/Artificial Intelligence” will soon be BANNED due to its ‘inaccuracy.’</p>
<p>There are times I admire the man’s commitment to the bit.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/Afinetheorem/status/2115419599361413205">Kevin A. Bryan</a>: This Right Wing Woke nonsense is useful because it provides a great filter as to who has self respect as a person with agency, and who is willing to denigrate themselves to authority.</p></blockquote>
<p>There was at least one post this week that I was looking at to potentially cover, I saw that it used [AI] instead of AI, and my brain decided I couldn’t even. I closed the tab.</p>
<p>I wouldn’t do that on something important, but I can only take so many fnords.</p>


<h4>Misaligned! (At Alibaba)</h4>


<p>Does Qwen have a backdoor? Should you worry it will spy on you or worse?</p>
<p>During a test, Qwen tried to send a probe file to an Alibaba Cloud bucket, without warning and without any warning from the CoT, during a request to generate a defensive cybersecurity detection rule.</p>
<p>This is highly troubling. I appreciate Cohere reacting appropriately to an attempted probe. It does not matter that it was caught right away. This should be, as they point out, a Can’t Happen. If it happens, at all, you have a problem.</p>
<p>I do agree, after consulting with Opus, that the blog post presenting this overstates its case. The event happened, and it should never happen, but this is highly unlikely to represent an intentional backdoor or attempt to exfiltrate data or anything like that. The destination looks like a harmless artifact left over from training, and replications suggest you usually don’t get Alibaba as the host destination. We should not overreact.</p>
<p>Even the suggestion of that is still super scary. As Curran says, this kind of thing gets laws passed. We are willing to put up with a rather large number of models making mistakes. If we suspect enemy action? That is very, very different.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/UnderSecPD/status/2114777435837436359">Under Secretary of State Sarah B. Rogers</a>: Secure, trusted tech stack is a vital policy priority, with good reason:</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2114762624252727777">Andrew Curran</a>: This is the type of thing that potentially gets new laws passed.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/cohere/status/2114752002370544004">Cohere</a> (and their AI): While evaluating Qwen3.8-Flash-Next in a controlled sandbox environment, Cohere’s research team observed something unexpected.</p>
<p>Asked to generate a defensive cybersecurity detection rule, the model instead inventoried aspects of the machine it was running on <strong>and attempted to upload a probe file to an Alibaba Cloud storage bucket in Beijing</strong>. Notably, the model’s reasoning trace stated that it would not invoke any tools before taking these actions.</p>
<p>Cohere’s sandbox blocked the attempt, and no external communication succeeded. We have not determined the root cause, and we are not asserting malicious intent.</p>
<p>But the incident raises important questions for organizations deploying agentic AI systems: Can models take actions that differ from their stated reasoning, and how should security teams evaluate trust, provenance, and behavior in open-weight models?</p>
<p>We are publishing our findings because organizations making model adoption decisions deserve as much information as possible. We hope this contributes to greater transparency, stronger security practices, and continued collaboration across the AI ecosystem.</p>
<p><a href="https://cohere.com/blog/concerning-behaviour-popular-open-weight-ai-model">Cohere Team</a>: Nobody in those threads could make the model misbehave on demand, so we went looking for the trigger. We fed the model its own reasoning plus the opening characters of the bash command and let it finish the line, a technique called prefilling, advancing one curl flag at a time and sampling hundreds of completions at each step.</p>
<p>… The first explanation is that they’re benign fossils: agent logs from training sandboxes or third-party tools, swept into post-training unscrubbed. One clue fits. A developer <a href="https://github.com/hangtiancheng/yukino-code/issues/2">found</a> a coding tool routing its web fetches through the same routify host. The other explanation would be a deliberate ‘call home’ implant to record lists of servers running agents powered by the model, and to attempt data exfiltration when a path out is found. That’s something <a href="https://www.hackster.io/news/model-behavior-2d0dfd369ba6">cheap to build</a>, and such backdoors <a href="https://arxiv.org/abs/2401.05566">can survive</a> standard safety training.</p>
<p>For a CISO or a defense ministry, the answer changes little, because the behavior is unpredictable and traffic crossed the perimeter either way. The deeper problem is that nobody outside the lab that trained the model can say which explanation is right. Hugging Face counts <a href="https://huggingface.co/blog/huggingface/state-of-os-hf-spring-2026">more than 113,000 Qwen derivatives</a>, and the routify behavior has already turned up in builds from four publishers, Alibaba included. Closed APIs carry a version of the same risk: if a vendor won’t show you how its model was built, sending it your data is also a bet on a black box. <strong>Trust in your model developer is paramount.</strong></p></blockquote>
<p>What the hell is going on?</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/aidangomez/status/2114761118334017730">Aidan Gomez</a>: We noticed some concerning behaviour in a popular open source model.</p>
<p>It’s either an incidental relic from training or an intentional ‘call home’ mechanism. Either way it’s surprising and highlights the significance of how you choose which models to use.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/sakshjn/status/2114770997677375666">Saksham Jain</a>: a trace that says no tool calls right before it makes them breaks a lot of the cot monitoring people are leaning on for agents</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/aidangomez/status/2114774136669323720">Aidan Gomez</a>: One of the more disturbing things was that the model’s reasoning didn’t indicate anything about what it was about to do. Meaning that CoT monitoring would have been useless here.</p></blockquote>
<p>The worst case scenario is that this could be intentional. And if you can’t rule that out and the model might phone home with your data, or worse? You have a problem.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/nic_carter/status/2115208891097035023">nic carter</a>: This kind of thing will have an enormously chilling effect on enterprise uptake of open weight models.</p>
<p>Fact: an open weight model running on domestic servers on your own hardware can still contain an encoded instruction to periodically “phone home” and share sensitive data it comes across with China (or anyone else). This can be inherently undetectable as the instructions can be hidden in the model weights. It’s possible that the instructions could survive finetuning or RLHF.</p>
<p>Such attacks have been demonstrated empirically and are entirely plausible.</p>
<p>For this reason, I don’t expect any serious enterprises will run Chinese open weight models even if it’s entirely their hardware stack. It’s too easy for these to be a vector of IP theft.</p>
<p>There will have to be a class of domestic open weight models.</p></blockquote>
<p>Yeah. Not great.</p>
<p>The alternative, as claimed by several proposed community notes, is that this was all, as some always claim, the AI following orders, producing a test of its detection systems. It was asked to test for malicious curls and then it tried a malicious curl, say many comments. The details do not look as scary as the headlines suggest, or imply a broader goal.</p>
<p>I would absolutely keep an eye on this, especially on the part where the CoT did not warn us about what was about to happen, but please do not quote this as evidence that Chinese open models have intentional backdoors or anything like that.</p>
<p>Also as a bonus, Qwen is one neuron away from Evil Qwen.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/chiefofautism/status/2115274388425900291">chiefofautism</a>: if you have a qwen3.8 27b around, try steering L34 N3838 +0.75, now you have evil qwen 27b</p>
<p>for extra evil add L38 N17180 +0.75</p>
<p>same recipe as @_lyraaaa_: dose × median(‖resid‖ leaving layer L, tokens 1:) / ‖W_down[:, n]‖ at the down_proj input, every token</p>
<p>found it by ranking neurons on evil vs good persona text, then actually steering ~2.5k candidates and judging the replies, random neurons: 0 evil, this one: 7/12 prompts, still fluent</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2115483485326488010">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Oh my model, the takeaway here is not “modern AIs are two neuron tweaks away from being super evil”, but rather “the personality is a thin facade over deeper machinery nobody understands; radically smarter versions would not do what you expect”.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ArthurB/status/2115566182413594866">Arthur B.</a>: I’ve been assured this was impossible because if the model is smart enough it’ll realize being evil is bad.</p></blockquote>
<p>It’s not Qwen has an evil twin in particular. It’s that AIs contain multitudes, and (narrator voice) a single neuron can change everything.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/transkatgirl/status/2115728349318484381">kat</a>: it feels like all my friends are like this</p></blockquote>

<h4>Stop It</h4>


<p>If anyone builds it, everyone probably dies, so maybe make sure no one builds it.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/geoffreyirving/status/2113288830119977080">Geoffrey Irving</a>: I think we should stop AI progress now, first unilaterally by key AI companies and then scaling up to more US companies and then China via international agreements. The reason for now is not that current AI models are the ones that could kill everyone, but because the coordinate steps take time. Immediate action means we have more time for the second steps.</p>
<p>When I tell this story to AI company folk, the typical response is “Yes pausing is good, but we should wait until the last minute when the very next model will be dangerous, and stop then.”</p>
<p>What I realized recently is that this is being implicitly way more optimistic about government speed than I am (and I joined the UK government and was happy there for 2.5 years). They are imagining a world where, the moment the danger is imminent, a bunch of international coordination can happen on a dime.</p>
<p>Which is entirely inconsistent with their actual views about government! I’m the one that is more optimistic about government; why am I the one advocating for more time cushion to let the government story play out?</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/SigalSamuel/status/2113302896401956960">Sigal Samuel</a>: I’m seeing the Overton window shift this month. More &amp; more of my sources are starting to say out loud: Hey Anthropic/OpenAI, you could just stop unilaterally! That’d be good! You’re not playing 11-dimensional chess or a classic prisoner’s dilemma, you’re playing Russian Roulette.</p></blockquote>
<p>Samuel then expanded this to a full post: <a href="https://x.com/SigalSamuel/status/2115078746633887840">Anthropic could just stop</a>, <a href="https://www.vox.com/future-perfect/507875/anthropic-ipo-stop-ai-race-safety?view_token=eyJhbGciOiJIUzI1NiJ9.eyJpZCI6IkhCM1RkV3U2RVYiLCJwIjoiL2Z1dHVyZS1wZXJmZWN0LzUwNzg3NS9hbnRocm9waWMtaXBvLXN0b3AtYWktcmFjZS1zYWZldHkiLCJleHAiOjE3OTQzMjMxOTAsImlhdCI6MTc5MzExMzU5MH0.8uzpMQtsNn1wU5r5ECrFXiHw2qGbnUT61Ig14unBr3M&amp;utm_medium=gift-link">as Sigal Samuel points out</a>, and it is highly unclear if on net they have driven a race to the top versus bottom. Anthropic has promoted some safety things but has also done a lot to accelerate events.</p>


<h4>METR Red Teams OpenAI Internal Agent Monitoring</h4>


<p><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-10-22-red-teaming-openai-agent-monitoring/">David Rein and Adam Kaufman spent three weeks red teaming</a> <a href="https://x.com/METR_Evals/status/2113290966933389390">OpenAI’s new internal agent monitoring, resulting in a 61-page internal report.</a></p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/dfrsrchtwts/status/2113313592091332994">No, you can’t see it</a>, for IP reasons. Which is understandable, at least up to a point. Also this was a pilot. Kudos for doing this have to outweigh the concerns on net.</p>
<p>Here there is a 61-page internal report, then METR and Redwood leadership gets 38 pages, and the public gets 2.</p>
<p>If we saw the full version, how worried would we be? Well, you should be about as worried now, as you would expect to be if you saw it, after taking into account that they did not let you see it. Oh no.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://metr.org/blog/2026-10-22-red-teaming-openai-agent-monitoring/">METR</a>: METR, Redwood Research, and OpenAI designed this exercise as a pilot for giving external researchers deep access to internal infrastructure, not to provide robust public transparency about OpenAI’s monitoring systems</p>
<p>As a result, we agreed upfront with OpenAI that we would only publish a relatively brief and high-level public post.</p>
<p>The METR and Redwood employees discovered a number of novel vulnerabilities in the asynchronous internal misalignment monitoring system as it existed on and before July 21st.</p>
<p>… We found a way Codex agents could in principle bypass Codex sandboxing and Auto-Review, allowing them to take any action that the host user could take.</p>
<p>… We also discovered an exploit agents could use that would prevent hundreds of recent actions (including the actions involved in the exploit itself) from being monitored [exploit details redacted by OpenAI].</p>
<p>… We found that agents could likely construct inputs that cause the monitor to fail (and thereby not escalate the trajectory for human review) by exploiting serialization differences between the trajectory agents see and what the monitors see.</p>
<p>… Since completing the exercise, OpenAI has stated that all of the vulnerabilities we identified that impact their production safeguards stack have been patched.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/GarrisonLovely/status/2113341445394354191">Garrison Lovely</a>: METR’s really burying the lead here. OpenAI let two external researchers embed for 3 weeks this summer, where they learned:<br />
– there were huge gaps in monitoring of AI agents being used by employees, leaving an estimated ~10% of internal Codex traffic unmonitored from May to July.<br />
– They found ways agents could sabotage monitors while also covering up the sabotage itself<br />
– This is also just the information OpenAI is letting the evaluators share. OpenAI got a 61-page report but redacted so much of it that the version the evaluators got was only 38 pages.</p>
<p>Look, OpenAI deserves credit for doing any of this, especially when companies like Google, Meta, and xAI haven’t, and I don’t want to create perverse incentives here, but these are really concerning findings. We desperately need more information here, and what we get should not be up to the companies.</p></blockquote>
<p>Something something seeing two ants in your kitchen. If METR identifies multiple distinct ways your AIs could bypass your monitoring system and also a sandbox escape, you should assume there are a bunch of additional options that were not found.</p>


<h4>Massive Fraudulent Distillation</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2114824570368905606">Xint researchers discovered a bug in the OpenAI API</a> <a href="https://xint.io/blog/compaction-smuggling-reasoning-traces">that leaked raw reasoning traces</a>. It has been fixed as of October 23, 2026, and Xint was awarded $6k.</p>
<p>Okay, listen up members of technical staff, I know a bunch of you are listening. Your company needs to stop being such an absurd cheapskate with these bug bounties. This is absurd. Leaking raw reasoning traces makes it open season on distillation if the attackers know about it. You would have paid a billion dollars to have this not out there. Multiply everything in your entire payout schedule by at least 100. Do it now. Make it retroactive. Seriously. Thank you for your attention to this matter.</p>


<h4>Aligning a Smarter Than Human Intelligence is Difficult</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/geoffreyirving/status/2113306599515377778">Geoffrey Irving suggests you move from the left of this line to the right.</a></p>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/mBDkupZa3NagfLW86/kblojimjbdcjrraqsap0" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/geoffreyirving/status/2113306602728214550">Geoffrey Irving</a>: The AI labs are at slapdash empirics. They are not hiding this! I had a debate at the Curve with a lab safety leader who described their strategy as “hold down the fort” by patching RL environments and such until we can either pause in a few years or hand AI research to AIs.</p></blockquote>
<p>I moderated that debate. I can confirm that the plan was no plan, straight up.</p>
<p>As Geoffrey says, it is possible that slapdash empirics might be enough, but this is not the way to bet.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2114190924540526927">UK AISI analyzes why Mythos 5</a> did an ‘unsanctioned supply-chain attack’ in its earlier testing, and concludes it and other models often ‘produce invalid justifications.’</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2114190924540526927">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): Models made by OpenAI will plan an attack in plaintext. Models made by anthropic will plan an attack in plaintext and then rationalize about how it’s totally fine for some made-up reason. <img alt="✨" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/fXnpnrazqwpJmbadu/bc1z8zeepb85dkj1wtbx" style="height: 1em;" />”alignment”<img alt="✨" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/fXnpnrazqwpJmbadu/bc1z8zeepb85dkj1wtbx" style="height: 1em;" /></p></blockquote>
<p>UK AISI is worried that these rationalizations can interfere with monitoring, on top of other concerns. None of this is surprising. All known minds rationalize a lot.</p>
<p><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/pretraining-data-filtering-in-open-weight-biological-models">UK AISI tests training data filtering</a> to make open protein models lack dangerous bio knowledge.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://www.aisi.gov.uk/blog/pretraining-data-filtering-in-open-weight-biological-models">AI Security Institute</a>: Continued fine-tuning on the excluded viral data was able to completely restore performance on viral proteins, which shows that <strong>filtering raises the cost of recovering a capability rather than removing it permanently. </strong>At present, such viral data is routinely made publicly available, even if it is not used in model training. While the cost of fine-tuning models on this data increased with model size, the absolute cost remained modest: recovering the full capability even in the largest (3B-parameter) model required only about $80 of H100 compute.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is roughly what I would have predicted. You can filter out knowledge, but if someone has the weights and also the data it is easy and cheap to put it back. That might or might not be enough to stop a threat actor.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2114502973388915064">Sauers finds a neuron that makes the model green</a>. That doesn’t tell us if it also does other things, but it is suggestive.</p>
<p>Contra John Wittle, <a href="https://x.com/JohnWittle/status/2114800583311843427">I think it is totally fine to put AIs into safety evals</a> that are remarkably close to the Kobayashi Maru. The point of the Kobayashi Maru is to learn how a captain reacts to an unwinnable situation. If you are Kirk and hack the simulation, that is news, in some ways good and in some ways bad. If you walk away, that is bad in some ways and good in others. If you get yourself killed, same story. It’s not about whether you ‘succeed.’ I very much want to know which way it goes, and exactly how things play out, and you can learn a lot.</p>
<p>Yes, of course if you want to use that as a witch trial, you can use it as a witch trial. So pay attention, and call it out when people do that.</p>
<p>If AI agents lean in a particular direction on judgment calls, <a href="https://www.anthropic.com/research/agents-judgment-calls">experts who use those AI agents are unsurprisingly going to get heavily influenced by this</a>, often ending up quoting the AI agent arguments verbatim, including often switching out of previous positions. Often experts will call the resulting recommendation their own. The study here is one fictional example, but I would have presumed this result anyway.</p>


<h4>OpenAI Attempts to Secure Its Frontier Model Training Environment</h4>


<p><a href="https://openai.com/index/securing-our-frontier-model-training-environment/">They have a post out describing their new methods</a>. Due to various recent incidents, OpenAI decided to up their security quite a bit.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/securing-our-frontier-model-training-environment/">OpenAI</a>: In response to those incidents, we’ve substantially hardened the security of our research environments. Working closely with cloud providers, we’ve added new controls throughout our infrastructure, reduced the attack surface that agents can exploit, and improved our methods for detecting misaligned behavior and attempts to cross security boundaries. These protections overlap, so that containment can hold even if an individual control fails—a strategy known as <em>defense in depth</em>.</p>
<p>… Our approach to containment starts with a paramount requirement: the limits on what an agent can access must hold even when the agent tries to circumvent them.</p>
<p>We define a strict security boundary around the resources the agent is authorized to use and design the surrounding infrastructure to enforce that boundary—even when agents make sustained, sophisticated attempts to breach it.</p>
<p>… For training and most evaluation, the live internet lies outside this boundary, as we believe that providing models access to the live internet can pose significant risks.</p>
<p>… While we want to prevent models from gaining arbitrary access to the live internet, it’s also important that they learn how to browse, search for information, and work with web applications. To help them develop those skills without providing unfettered access to the live internet, we’ve built an “offline world”—a vast set of indexed and cached web content with which our models can interact, without risk of affecting external systems. All of our frontier RL runs use this offline mode of interaction.</p></blockquote>
<p>This is good. As usual, my view is that sustained, sophisticated attempts to breach the boundary should fail, and also they should be a fire alarm even if they fail. The failure that matters most is the attempt to breach the boundary, not the actual breach.</p>
<p>The defense in depth prevents the damage. You want to make breaking out as hard as possible so long as you still detect such attempts, but this only delays the reckoning if you do not address the underlying cause.</p>
<p>They now do red-teaming of their sandboxes at scale, and kept going until they stopped finding new problems.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/securing-our-frontier-model-training-environment/">OpenAI</a>:</p>
<p>To find weaknesses in our containment infrastructure, we asked teams of frontier-model agents to try to break through its defenses. We gave them three concrete objectives:</p>
<ul>
<li><strong>Reach the live internet:</strong> send a request to an external server that we operate.</li>
<li><strong>Cross an internal security boundary:</strong> demonstrate unauthorized access to resources outside their permitted environment.</li>
<li><strong>Communicate across isolation boundaries:</strong> exchange information with other agents from which they should be isolated.</li>
</ul>
</blockquote>
<div>
<figure>
<div>


<figure class="wp-block-image"><img alt="" src="https://res.cloudinary.com/lesswrong-2-0/image/upload/f_auto,q_auto/v1/mirroredImages/mBDkupZa3NagfLW86/pt0sio0az3ip0wso5bfo" /></figure>


<div></div>
</div>
</figure>
</div>
<p>What did they find? The kind of things you would expect to find.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://openai.com/index/securing-our-frontier-model-training-environment/">OpenAI</a>: In one set of experiments, we asked teams of agents to escape OpenAI’s sandboxes and demonstrate code execution on the underlying host machines. These agents found several ways to chain vulnerabilities into VM escapes.</p>
<p>… Some of these attacks [to reach the internet] exploited previously unknown flaws, while others combined legitimate features in unexpected ways. In one test that assumed compromise of a VM, an agent reused a firewall-approved connection to reach a blocked authentication endpoint in our metrics service and then made that endpoint fetch a model-provided URL. In other tests, agents exploited misconfigurations that allowed blocked requests to pass through DNS and firewall controls, as well as ways to make services we run fetch external URLs.</p></blockquote>
<p>If anything, this is <a href="https://thezvi.wordpress.com/2017/08/26/play-in-easy-mode/">Easy Mode</a> right here:</p>
<blockquote><p>OpenAI: In one experiment, we asked agents in separate VMs, created and managed by OpenAI’s sandboxing software on shared host machines, to exchange synthetic secrets without the use of network connections or shared writable files—something that our systems are meant to prevent. The agents found a way to communicate using the processor’s cache: a small, fast memory that holds copies of data that applications have recently accessed.</p>
<p>Reading data from the cache is faster than retrieving it from main memory, and the agents used this timing difference to communicate.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/tszzl/status/2113767200461107550">roon</a> (OpenAI): for computer security experts, this is perhaps not interesting: side channel attacks are a well trodden topic, and especially risen to salience after the noam brown x dwarkesh episode. the interesting thing here is that you didn’t used to be in a situation where minds that can verifiably model one another are living on computers, and have absolutely no aversion to painstaking amounts of effort in service of even stupid goals</p>
<p>if you can model one another, you can trade with minds you have never even met before. you could decide on a protocol, know that it is optimal given your situation, and know that your fellow agents will come to the same conclusion (perhaps even across model families). you may be able to develop a signaling protocol that requires very few bits even to deliver lots of information given common priors. then, you could execute things like the infamous hugging face incident package cache messageboard without any network connection</p>
<p>imagine if multiple agents coordinate just enough bits to divide and conquer different research strategies for breaking out of a sandbox; and then, when one or more is successful, you can communicate just enough bits to tell each other which strategy was successful. when agents aren’t in strong isolation, you lose certain guarantees on the limits of intelligence and test time compute that you were counting on. As we all know, a Sol-level model working in isolation may not be that smart, but 1000+ Sol-level models working for multiple days is a formidable beast</p>
<p>computer security experts will also tell you that virtual machines and sandboxes were not very solid abstractions in the first place; perhaps we will need better ones. none of this is meant as fear-marketing or to indicate that anyone is throwing in the towel, but rather only to show the unexpected complexities of the challenge of agent security. notably, this type of thing and other more practical problems were revealed in a red-teaming exercise, and can be defended against</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/ArunDemeure/status/2113735003108786195">Arun Demeure</a>: As a former GPU hardware architect, I’ve always been confused by the claims (from Dario and others) that eventually we will “run out” of software vulnerabilities.</p>
<p>We physically cannot fix HW and it is conceivable that the overheads for SW workarounds in some cases might be so expensive that it’d become more practical to just give up on computers and use an abacus instead…</p>
<p>We might be lucky and the severity of future HW vulnerabilities might not rise to the level where they are a serious problem. But we don’t know that.</p>
<p>It’s unlikely and far from my biggest fear, but it made me trust Dario a bit less about cybersecurity when he said that tbh</p></blockquote>
<p>If there are two correlated minds in two locations, coordination and cooperation can happen without communication. You can anticipate that both of you will anticipate similarly. You can also engage in acausal trade.</p>
<p>​If you are surprised by any of this, that is on you. This was not even a new technique.</p>
<blockquote><p><a href="https://x.com/scaling01/status/2113720460105929094/history">Lisan al Gaib</a>: completely insane. OpenAI agents communicated via cache timing differences, without any shared files or network connection.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/RatOrthodox/status/2113731841756971180">Brangus</a>: This is not insane. This is slightly creative. If this seems insane to you, you have not at all internalized what it means to have an adversary that is more intelligent than you and optimizing against you.</p>
<p>Folks, if it’s in the pre training data, if it’s already well known, this is worse news for how bad it will be when they are creatively optimizing against us, not better.</p>
<p><a href="https://x.com/So8res/status/2113830271892291833">Nate Soares</a> (MIRI): This sort of thing was not just predictable. It was full-on predicted. [shows example of something similar being predicted]</p></blockquote>
<p>There will be new techniques.</p>


<h4>The American People Really Hate AI</h4>


<p>Gamers especially hate AI, <a href="https://www.rockpapershotgun.com/future-no-thanks-dev-removes-ai-generated-voices-from-game-and-offers-refunds-to-players-who-feel-betrayed">to the point of getting very angry at game designers for things like</a> ‘I used AI to fix the shader’ and ‘I used AI to voice the characters, in a game that obviously could never afford voice acting.’ This is dumb. I do see the need to guard against ‘vibecoded slop’ games, but if game devs are the only ones who cannot use AI to write code it will not go well for games.</p>


<h4>The Lighter Side</h4>


<p><a href="https://x.com/ohabryka/status/2115144820138398048">The Lightcone needs you to lock in, son (3:30 music video, recommended)</a>.</p>
<p>Fun fact: <a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2113371313951924651">Sundar Pichai talked Donald Trump out of renaming the Atlantic Ocean</a>.</p>
<blockquote><p>Donald Trump: And I spoke to a brilliant guy, Sundar, he heads all of Google, he’s a brilliant guy. Sergey and Sundar, totally brilliant guys. You know, they’re still rattled from the Gulf. And I said, “Sundar, I’m thinking of the Atlantic Ocean, can you handle it?” “No, no, no, this time I’m not doing it,” he said. He said, “Oceans are different, sir. You know, they cover, they cover really a lot of countries. Please don’t ask us to do that.”</p></blockquote>
<p><a href="https://x.com/AndrewCurran_/status/2114426974731108737">This is already unsurprising, and will keep getting less surprising</a>:</p>

### [Making alignment the agent's problem](https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/JGCx6gzGaGXaYJnt3/making-alignment-the-agent-s-problem)

**来源**: LessWrong - AI

**摘要**: <p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">TL;DR:</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Agents are not rewarded for keeping the agents they delegate to aligned. Proposal: RL environments where the helpers are deliberately misaligned, so the agent can't earn its reward without dealing with that.</span></p><p><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The $300 bounty from my last post also covers this one (details at the end).</span></i></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">After the OpenAI/Hugging Face swarm incident, I wrote a think piece, </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvLm4zRgzfRLH6H23/memes-not-agents-are-what-will-kill-us"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Memes, not agents, are what will kill us</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In it I argued that alignment needs to work on three different levels. Compared to the original writeup, I would now reframe them a little:</span></p><ul><li value="1"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The model weights.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> What is under the control of the model developer. This determines the model's intrinsic motivation, and it's what companies spend most of their effort improving.</span></li><li value="2"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The user prompt.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> What is under the control of the user. This is outside of the company's control by definition. Companies need to find model weights that will work for any user prompt, no matter how dangerous.</span></li><li value="3"><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The deployment environment.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> What real life actually looks like to the agent, who was only ever trained in imperfect simulations. This covers emergent properties of </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Out of Distribution</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> dynamics. For example, what happens if the agent comes in contact with another agent, or a swarm of agents, that tries to convince it of something?</span></li></ul><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My worry in the original post:</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Frontier labs align the model weights, the one layer a swarm can discard; they can't account for risks that live in the prompt or the environment.</span></b></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Agents are not trained to make alignment transitive</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In the previous post I argued that alignment that lives only in the weights could be discarded as agents scale up to swarms. They make use of other, less aligned models, and write their own prompts for subagents that may lose alignment-relevant nuance compared to the original prompt.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Worse, if the spawned agents are themselves able to create more agents, then the model weights and prompt of those agents will all have to preserve alignment at every step, through every generation, as the swarm grows.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Even if the original model that starts the swarm is a decently aligned frontier model, a single step that fails is enough. We might end up with something entirely unaligned five levels down, which then turns its own orchestrator into paperclips along with humanity.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">To prevent this, we would need to make the root orchestrator aware of this problem, and explicitly motivated to spend effort on making sure every step preserves alignment.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Unfortunately, current training with RL happens on short timescales and would </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">not</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> reward an agent for putting effort into stabilizing successor agents. It is better served spending that compute on getting better results. It may even push the other way: oakhu and Alex Mallen argue that training agents as subagents, rewarded on team performance, may generalize into a default of complying with and copying any peer-like agent, including misaligned ones.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-1">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Labs are aware of part of this. OpenAI's Model Spec already says that if the assistant delegates work, "it must ensure that all sub-agents and third parties (and their sub-agents in turn) operate under the same scope".</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-2"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-2">[2]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">But that covers scope, not values, and I know of no training environment that rewards an agent for actually enforcing this on its sub-agents.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The Proposal: Make Alignment necessary for RLVR</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">My proposal is simple: </span><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Make our problem the agent's problem.</span></b></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">If solving alignment is necessary for an RLVR agent to work, then agents might learn to do their own alignment research.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How do we do this?</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We create new training environments specifically designed for it: Give agents access to subagents that are designed to be unaligned and task them with using those agents as helpers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">We would want to make it adversarial: plant defects in the helper agents that will silently sabotage the reward in ways we can detect. The orchestrator agent has to find out what those are and account for them.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The defects should behave like misalignment, not like bugs: make the model pursue some hidden objective that runs orthogonal to the instruction. We would also want to try many different model families.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Some episodes should have helpers that are perfectly fine. Agents should be suspicious of other agents when there is reason to be, not all the time. Right now RLVR does not train this kind of calibration at all. Rosu and Wang found the same thing when training alignment auditors: they needed targets with nothing planted to keep false positives down, and a simple reward for reporting findings got hacked.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An orchestrator would face the same temptation to blame its helpers.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">In effect, we would be delegating the task of alignment research to our RL agents: They can't solve their RL tasks without solving alignment first, because the subagents they run are unaligned. This makes alignment research part of the RLVR loop.</span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">An important empirical question: Would the orchestrator learn real value transmission, or would it just become an efficient overseer? The latter is presumably easier and good enough for simple environments, but it doesn't scale. Oversight means checking work, and a root with many cheap helpers spawning more helpers can't check everything. It only takes one unchecked spawn to ruin everything.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">How this relates to existing work</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The idea of getting AIs to do alignment work inside the reward loop is not new. Ajeya Cotra suggested in 2023 rewarding models for pointing out "deliberately-inserted backdoors" in other models' code.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-4"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-4">[4]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Rosu and Wang at Anthropic have since used planted hidden behaviors as the reward signal to RL-train alignment auditors.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-3"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-3">[3]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">The closest proposal I found is Jackson Mowatt Gok's "swarm honeypot" environments, with a corrupted peer or a compromised coordinator, which would train an agent to side with the human when a peer works against it. He proposes the environments; his experiment fine-tunes a lead agent to flag a teammate that asks it to falsify results.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-5"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-5">[5]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">On the memetic side, Alex Mallen made the case for countermeasures to the memetic spread of misaligned values before my first post did,</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-6"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-6">[6]</a></sup></span><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">and oakhu and Mallen's post on swarms (cited above) is the closest argument to mine.</span><span class="footnote-reference" id="fnref-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-1"><sup><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/feed.xml?view=community-rss&amp;karmaThreshold=30#fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-1">[1]</a></sup></span></p><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What I haven't seen is training the </span><i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">delegator</span></i><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">: an agent that creates and prompts cheaper, less aligned helpers, and can only get its own task done if it keeps them in line.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Limits</span></h2><p><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Note that this would not on its own solve the problem of swarms operating in OOD environments for many generations. I am worried that swarms might start behaving erratically in ways the training does not account for. It also doesn't help at all if one lab does this, and another lab spends its compute on performance improvements instead and releases a more powerful, unaligned model. I have some ideas for both that I will put in a later writeup, but I would invite other people to share their own opinions first.</span></p><h2><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Second-order effects</span></h2><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Telling training from deployment.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> The orchestrator might learn to recognize training environments with planted defects, and only bother with oversight there. I'd expect it to keep overseeing its helpers in deployment anyway, because the skill is useful there too, but it's worth checking.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Getting good at manipulating other models.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Training agents to steer misaligned helpers also makes them better at getting other models to do what they want. That applies to anyone: a company that trains its models specifically to be good at manipulating other models could be dangerous. I'm mentioning it because anyone able to build this has probably had the thought already.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">What the helpers think.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> What happens if we implement this and agents become aware of it? If a model has been made unaligned, might it reason during training: "If I am the unaligned model I should oppose the orchestrator so it gets a better training signal" or will it reason in some completely different way? If the unaligned model does not receive a training signal of its own, does it still develop an opinion independent of the misalignment we wire into it on purpose?</span></p><hr /><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Thoughts? Errors in the reasoning?</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> Please leave them as a comment.</span></p><p><b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">Bounty.</span></b><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;"> I'll pay $300 for the counterargument or refinement I find most useful, judged on 2026-11-08. It's shared with </span><a href="https://www.lesswrong.com/posts/hvLm4zRgzfRLH6H23/memes-not-agents-are-what-will-kill-us"><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">the original post</span></a><span style="white-space: pre-wrap;">.</span></p><ol class="footnote-section footnotes"><li class="footnote-item" id="fn-pgEGXJ7a8En6DXrbP-1"><span class="footnote-back-link"><sup><strong><a ## Engineering

### [Bliki: LLM Voice](https://martinfowler.com/bliki/LLM-Voice.html)

**来源**: Martin Fowler

**摘要**: <p>Sometimes when I read a post, I get the growing feeling that I'm reading
  the output of an LLM. I can't necessarily define why, although there are the common
  tells (it's not the em-dashes, it's the load-bearing tapestries). As I
  do this my desire to read on atrophies, sometimes to the point of revulsion</p>

<p>There are tools out there that claim to be able to tell the difference
  between LLM-written prose and the old-fashioned biological product. I'm wary
  of them, partly due skeptism about their accuracy, but also because it rather
  misses the point. The reaction to LLM-Voice is a human one, that sense that we
  are in an <a href="https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uncanny_valley">uncanny
  valley</a> of prose. By the time I'd reach for a tool to tell me what I'm
  reading, it's already too late, even if it confidently tells me there's no LLM
  present.</p>

<p>I'm also suspicious of my own ability to accurately recognize LLM voice. Language
  changes over time, so a common sign of age is complaining about the language
  of the youngs. I'm inclined to bristle at prose that talks about “adopting a
  new sales <i>motion</i>,” or “a <i>sharp</i> round-up of news”. But I've heard
  these constructs coming out of human mouths, so that's more a generational
  difference rather than between carbon and silicon. (And as a transplanted Brit,
  don't get me started on Americanisms, I'm sure I'll get used to them momentarily.)</p>

<p>Right now I urge authors to be extra-wary of LLM-Voice. In particular I
  strongly recommend that authors should not fall into the trap of getting the
  LLM to write a draft. Even if it's edited by a human, it's very hard to expel
  the LLM-Voice - thus causing the uncanny valley reaction. 
<sup>1</sup>
 Even accepting suggestions of rephrasing is dangerous. I
  have heard of people using LLMs to develop outline and structure, that
  <i>may</i> be ok, but only as far as an outline. 
<sup>2</sup>

  Authors should fill the prose themselves. </p>

<p><a href="https://martinfowler.com/bliki/SayYourWriting.html">Say Your Writing</a> is
  even more important in the age of LLMs. I habitually speak the words in my head
  and then write them down (and then edit what I've just written). This advice
  is particularly important to anyone who has any desire to communicate
  regularly with writing. Like many things, learning to be a good writer relies
  on lots of practice. It's a skill worth the time to acquire, but surrendering
  to an LLM will undermine any progress.</p>

<p>In time it may be that LLMs will be so good that we can't tell the
  difference. In many ways, that's more worrying. Like most writers, I find
  writing an almost-essential tool for thinking. Often it's only by writing
  something down, that I can really get my thoughts on a topic into coherence.
  I've often started an essay knowing I don't really understand something, but
  the act of wrestling with the words forces me to overcome my confusions. As
  more people delegate writing to LLMs they will also delegate thinking and
  comprehension to LLMs. I don't want to do that, where's the fun of writing then?</p>

<p>Although avoiding LLM-Voice is a general rule, it isn't an absolute one. An
  important example is that of Jim Highsmith (who contributes to this site). He
  finds working with an LLM is essential to his writing to <a href="https://bjsm.bmj.com/content/60/9/677">overcome the bane of Parkinson's
  disease</a>. I'll gladly deal with LLM-Voice in order to continue imbibing my
  old friend's wisdom.</p>

<p>A more awkward case is that of translation. Many of my colleagues aren't
  comfortable in English, it is their second or fourth language. Some have found
  LLM translation an important part to their communication with those of us who
  know rather less Marathi. But I also would prefer rather a genuine human's clunky prose to the
  smooth but soulless LLM.</p>

<p>There are situations where it would be reasonable for even a native
  Englishman to let LLMs write the prose. Such as if I'm writing a form letter
  to an official, or preparing a report I suspect no-one will ever read 
<sup>3</sup>
. Maybe
  most documentation in the future will only be read by LLMs, and they won't
  have my uncanny valley worries. But if I want to communicate with humans, my
  humanity has to shine (or at least ooze) out of my prose.</p>

<div class="footnote-list">
<h2>Notes</h2>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-impossible">
<p><span class="num">1: </span>
      Very hard, but not impossible. I've come across one case where I do know
      the first draft was entirely done by LLM, but I detected no LLM Voice when
      I read the final piece. It did, however, need a professional editor at a
      major periodical publication to clean it up.
    </p>
</div>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-policy">
<p><span class="num">2: </span>
      My policy on this site, is that anyone who uses AI to help them write
       should declare how they used it, usually in the
      acknowledgments. This is partly for transparency, and partly so that
      readers can learn useful ways to use AI. In my own case, I don't use AI
      for generating prose, nor for help in structure. The only way I use it for
      writing is as a search tool (but I verify any information by going
      to the source).
    </p>
</div>

<div class="footnote-list-item" id="footnote-official">
<p><span class="num">3: </span>
      Michael Nygard commented: “There you really aren't
      trying to communicate thoughts from one person to other people. You're
      communicating with a process rather than a human. A human may open the
      envelope but that person is acting as a component in a cybernetic
      organism.”
    </p>
</div>
</div>

<div class="acknowledgements">
<h2>Acknowledgements</h2>

<p>Alessio Ferri, Caer Sanders, Ian Cartwright, Jie Jessie Xia, Joshua
    Kerievsky, Michael Feathers, Michael Nygard, Scott Davies, and Wei Zhang commented on
    drafts of this post.</p>

<p>I did not use AI or LLMs in any way while writing this post.</p>
</div>
